A box's `[:where(&>*)]:` classes style each of its direct child parts, frames and instances alike. Tailwind compiles them to `:where(.… > *)`, at (0,0,0), so a part's own class beats them, as an avatar's own ring would beat the one its Avatar group gives every avatar. The build reaches parts only, so a box that also holds a direct icon would leave that icon without the class. A box's `**:` classes style every element below it, at any depth: each part and each icon of the box and of its parts. Tailwind compiles them to `:is(.… *)`, which weighs the class and its variants, so a vertical Navigation menu trigger's `data-popup-open:**:text-accent-foreground` (0,2,0) beats its caret's own `text-muted-foreground` (0,1,0), and its `enabled-hover:**:` arm (0,1,0) beats it by its later place in Tailwind's output. The box itself and its own text, a text node and no element, keep the box's color. An ancestor's `**:` reaches the root and everything below it. A `**:` class that no element below its box takes in any cell drops, as the row link's does around its bare text, and one that reaches the element a box holds its label in stays unmapped, since the kit draws no layer for that element. A variant after `**:` stays unmapped too. A class that styles every child of its box but the last, as Tailwind compiles `space-y-4` to `:where(.space-y-4 > :not(:last-child))`, drops on a box that holds one element child, since `:last-child` matches that child, as Pagination's root holds its one row. Every element child counts, drawn or not, and the capture records each one, as the visually hidden `<span>` of Breadcrumb's ellipsis. Between two or more children it sets a margin the kit does not draw, so it stays unmapped, and on an element around the root it drops with that element's reason. A negative `space-x-*` in a left-to-right flex row is the exception. Each child but the last ends that far early, so each child starts that far over the one before, and a later sibling paints over an earlier one (CSS Flexbox 1 §5.4, §9.4). On 2026-10-23 a real file drew a negative `itemSpacing` the same way, the later layer on top (N7). So `-space-x-2` draws as an item spacing of -8, as the Avatar group stacks its avatars. That holds only where every element child is a part in the row's flow with no margin of its own, which would beat the class's `:where()`, beside no icon, label or gap. Anywhere else, and for any other `space-*`, the class stays unmapped. `color: currentcolor` computes to the color the part inherits, so the checkbox indicator's `text-current` keeps the root's `primary-foreground`, and the coverage table records the class with what it resolved to. A flex item sits in no line box, so a part's `vertical-align` drops in a flex box (CSS Flexbox 1 §3), as each avatar's `align-middle` does in an Avatar group. A flex box's `flex-direction` sets its direction. `row`, or none, makes a horizontal auto layout, and `column` a vertical one. Any other direction stays unmapped. A part's alignment comes from its own classes. `items-start`, `items-center` and `items-end` set the counter axis to MIN, CENTER or MAX. `justify-start`, `justify-center` and `justify-end` set the primary axis, and `justify-between` sets it to SPACE_BETWEEN, which puts the first and last layer flush with the ends and the spare room between the layers. A box that hugs its layers along its direction, with no minimum there, has no spare room, so CSS packs them at its start with its gap between them (CSS Box Alignment 3 §8), and the build writes MIN with the gap, as a Select trigger hugs its value and caret. A box of fixed or filling length has no spare room either where one of its layers fills it along its direction, since that layer takes the free space (CSS Flexbox 1 §9.7), and `space-between` places a lone layer as `flex-start` does (CSS Box Alignment 3 §5.3), so both write MIN with the gap, as a filling Select trigger packs its growing value and its caret and Select field's heading row its lone label. Figma's SPACE_BETWEEN would drop the gap beside a filling layer (PV), and MIN keeps it: a 384px row drew a filling text 0..364 and a 16px frame 368..384 with its bound 4px gap (W5-P3). The flow leaves such a box's `space-between` unread until its layers map, and the box mapping reads it then, so its coverage row says whether the box spaces its layers out, holds one layer or packs beside one that grows. A layer that a BOOLEAN property hides still counts, since SPACE_BETWEEN packs the lone visible layer at the start while it is hidden (W5-P1). In a flex column, a layer whose negative top margin is exactly the column's gap sits flush under the layer before it, since a flex item's margins add to the gap (CSS Flexbox 1 §4.2), so the column draws the two in a frame with no gap, named `flush`, that takes their place, as Radio group's description sits under its legend (`components/flush-layers.ts`). A wrapping row whose items the capture measured on one line in every cell drops `flex-wrap`, since what fits depends on its text and a Figma row never wraps. A wrapping row of a fixed height whose items the capture measured on one line in every mode (`oneFlexLine`) lays out as a row that does not wrap: CSS collects the items into one line (CSS Flexbox 1 §9.3 step 1), and `align-content: normal` stretches that line to the row's height (§9.4), the one line a row that does not wrap has, so its items stretch and centre across as there, as a Date range picker's inputs `self-stretch` down its field box. The row's `align-content` must stretch, and its auto margins take that line's room, which `freeAlongMain` measures with `nowrap` forced. The capture reads the row's items through each `display: contents` child, and takes the text between two boxes, across comments and such children, as one item, as CSS wraps it in one anonymous item (CSS Flexbox 1 §4). A run of document white space alone makes no item, and a run of nonbreaking spaces does. It records no fact where the row or such a child has an in-flow `::before` or `::after`, or a run of text sets more than one line, since the page reads no box for either. It records no room in a column that holds text, since the box around a run's glyphs leaves out the leading of its line. An instance a designer narrows below the row wraps it in CSS and clips it in Figma, which is a known limit. The kit maps no `flex-wrap` to Figma's `WRAP`, since no capture shows where Figma breaks a line. Where a row's `flex-wrap` drops, its `row-gap` drops too, since it spaces only the lines a wrapping row breaks into (CSS Box Alignment 3 §8.1), and its `column-gap` spaces its layers, as the Command palette footer's `gap-x-4` sets its hints 16px apart. Figma's SPACE_BETWEEN drops the gap: on 2026-10-23 a real file put a filling text against the caret beside it (PV), and the owner's Select triggers drew their caret against the value. Any other alignment stays unmapped. In a row an unset alignment stays centered, as every published row was drawn, except where CSS shows the start it packs the layers at (CSS Box Alignment 3 §5.1.3). A row of fixed width, or one that fills its parent, packs its layers at its start beside a layer that hugs its content, as Select's item text hugs its label, and a filling row does so when every in-flow layer has a fixed width and none is a direct icon layer, a scope the kit keeps rather than a CSS condition, as Data table filter's bar sets its Trigger at the start of its 768px row. A part's own `align-self` places it across a row in place of the row's `align-items`. Figma aligns all of a row's layers one way, so a row whose parts ask for one alignment takes it, as Item's media and content top the row beside a description. A part that asks for none stretches across the row's height where it draws the same stretched: a flex row that sets no height or margin, paints nothing, pads the same above and below, and places its own layers across as the row's `align-items` would place it, as Item's `flex items-center` actions do. On 2026-10-23 a real file stretched a frame that filled down a hugging row to the height the row's other layers set, and kept the row hugging (PF), so the stretch draws what CSS draws only where the part is no taller than those layers. The build bounds each layer's height once the row's parts map, from fixed lengths, paddings, gaps and in-layout strokes, line heights and the lines a text can set, and stretches the part only where its tallest height is no taller than the shortest height of the tallest layer that sets an alignment, as Item's actions, one 20px line, sit beside a content column of at least two lines. A length a variable sets spans the least and the greatest value the variable holds across its modes, and a percent line height that share of its font size's, so the bound holds at every density: Alert's action, a Button of `control-h-sm`, at most 36px, sits beside a title and a description of at least 44.25px, one 14px line each at 137.5% and 150% and the 4px between them. The stretched part then centers what it holds where CSS centers the part, and the row's `align-items` reads as literal through it. Where a layer cannot stretch so, the row cannot place it as CSS does, so the row's `align-items` stays unmapped, naming the layer. Two alignments, or a row that leaves its other layers to an unset `align-items`, leave `align-self` unmapped. The row tells its layers apart by their keys, so two instances of one set, which share the set's slot as their name, each keep their own alignment, height and stretch. A column takes CSS's start on both axes. Its unset `align-items` stretches its layers, so a part with no width fills the column's width, while a part whose width is a length or `w-fit` keeps it. `fit-content` is no wider than the column, though (CSS Sizing 3 §5.1), so a `w-fit` part that holds only its text, paints nothing and sets its text from its start fills the column instead, and its text fills and wraps at the column's width, as Item's title wraps when a designer types a long one. A column that hugs its width is as wide as its widest layer and stretches the others to it, while Figma stretches a filling layer only to the width the column's other layers set (PF). The build cannot tell which layer is widest, so a column that hugs its width does not stretch a part, and the build records the missing `align-items` as unmapped under the column's class. A flex box that hugs its length along its direction leaves no free space, so the one part with `flex: 1` keeps its own length, and Select's value hugs its text and drops its `flex-1`. A row that hugs above a least width, as a spanning box above, has room past its layers, and its `flex: 1` part stays unmapped, except in a row of three layers whose middle one grows between two that neither grow nor shrink, centers its text, takes equal margins and paints nothing of its own, as Calendar's heading sits between its month buttons. A middle layer with a background, border, outline or shadow keeps its `flex` unmapped, since CSS paints those across the whole width it grows to. There the text sits halfway between the other two layers at any width the row takes. A row that spaces out its layers, SPACE_BETWEEN with no item spacing, and the middle layer hugging inside a frame that pads it by its margin and the row's gap on each side, places the text there too, and is as wide as CSS's row at its least width and past it: Figma's SPACE_BETWEEN drops the gap (CAL2), so the padding holds it. In a box of definite length along its direction, the one part with `flex: 1` and no length of its own along it fills the room the others leave, which Figma's fill takes, as Item's content column fills what the media and the actions leave. A part with a length of its own along the direction fills the same way where it also sets its own minimum there: the zero basis sizes it in place of its length (CSS Flexbox 1 §9.2, step 3), and its minimum replaces the automatic one, which would read that length (§4.5), as Input group's control drops its `w-full` beside `flex-1 min-w-0`. Without a minimum of its own its `flex` stays unmapped. A part's `max-height: 100%` lowers its height to its parent's content height, the parent's fixed height less its border and padding (CSS 2.1 §10.5, §10.7). Where the part's own height is the parent's height variable, or literal pixels at least that content height, and it sets no minimum, it is exactly that tall, so it fills down, as Input group's control fills the field inside its border. Elsewhere the maximum stays unmapped. In a row its length in pixels, which a layer inside it reads, is the row's less the other layers and the gaps, and unknown where one of them is no literal length. A row that wraps drops its `flex-wrap` there: the part that grows starts from a zero basis, so the row breaks no line until the other layers and that part's narrowest content overflow it, and a Figma row never wraps. A flex row that hugs its width is as wide as its layers on one line (CSS Sizing 3 §5.1) only where nothing bounds the width CSS makes available to it, which the build tracks apart from the row's Figma sizing: every box around it, up to an in-flow root with no preview width, hugs its width with no `max-width`. A box of fixed or filling width around it narrows a hugging flex item to its own width (CSS Flexbox 1 §9.7) or caps a column's item at it (§9.4), a `max-width` caps what it holds, a grid sizes its item against its track (CSS Grid 1 §6.2), which the build does not read, and an absolute box shrinks to fit its containing block (CSS 2.1 §10.3.7), while Figma draws the row hugging its layers all the same. A row whose `width` does not map, such as `w-min`, hugs in Figma only in its place, so it proves nothing either. So the row's `flex-wrap` drops only where nothing bounds it, as on Breadcrumb's list and Pagination's row, and stays unmapped under any of those. In a hugging box whose minimum above zero or maximum along that direction can change its length, or beside a second part that grows, `flex` stays unmapped. Where the capture measured a box under its `max-height` in every cell, the box hugs as if uncapped, so its one growing part drops `flex`, as the Command palette's `flex-1` list does under its 380px cap. A grid with no template puts each child in a row of its own, so `grid` with `place-content-center` maps to a box centered on both axes only while it holds one layer, as the checkbox indicator holds its glyph. Any other grid stays unmapped. A `block` box with no layers lays nothing out, so it reads as a frame, as Switch's thumb does. One that holds only its label sets the text in lines across its width, which the label's own sizing draws, as Field's description does. CSS sets the first line at the top, so a block with a height or a height limit of its own keeps the label there. One that hugs its height is as tall as its lines and keeps the row's centering, which draws the same. One that holds only block-level parts, each a `block`, `flex` or `grid` box, or one block-level icon, as preflight makes an `<svg>`, and sets no gap, stacks them down a vertical auto layout from the top left with no gap, and so does one whose one part is an inline-level box with a height of its own that spans the box's one line (CSS 2.1 §9.2.2, §10.3.9): an inline-block as wide as it, as TextField's `relative` container holds its `<input>`, which Chromium measured exactly as tall as its line at both densities, or an inline-block, inline flex or inline grid box in a box that hugs its width, which shrinks to fit it, as a Pagination item holds its link, which Chromium measured exactly as tall as the link at both densities on 2026-11-17, the item first growing past it at a 32px line against its 24px one, as Accordion's item stacks its header and panel (CSS 2.1 §9.4.1). A part that sets no width is as wide as the box (CSS 2.1 §10.3.3), so it fills a box whose width is fixed or fills. A box that hugs its width gives its blocks no width to fill, unless it holds one, which it shrinks to fit exactly, a gap separates no blocks, and a part whose display nothing sets may be inline, so each of them leaves `display` unmapped. One with any other layer stays unmapped.

A box's `width` and `height` set the sizing of each axis. A length is fixed, and an unset one hugs, unless a column stretches it. A length of `auto`, written or read through a custom property, sizes the axis as an unset one does, as Item's icon media hugs its glyph through `h-(--item-media-line)` while `--item-media-line` is `auto`. `max-content` is as wide as what the box holds laid out on one line (CSS Sizing 3 §5.1), which a frame that hugs a hugging text draws, so a bar trigger's `w-max` hugs. Exactly `100%` fills the axis when the box's parent is a flex box whose length on that axis is definite: fixed, or itself filling a definite length. Along the parent's direction that holds only for the parent's only layer. Beside other layers, CSS sizes the box at the whole length and then shrinks or overflows it with them, while a fill takes only the room they leave, so such a `100%` stays unmapped, and its row names the other layers. In a parent that hugs the axis, the percentage takes a length that depends on the box, and Figma would shrink the box, so it stays unmapped. Down a parent that stacks its layers down, a column or a box of blocks, and hugs its height, a percentage height computes to `auto` instead (CSS 2.1 §10.5), so any share hugs, beside other layers too, as the Navigation menu popup's `size-full` viewport and `h-full` content do in a popup as tall as they are. `components/percent-height.ts` owns the rule. In a row that hugs its height the row's stretch decides the item, so the height stays unmapped there. Where nothing bounds the width CSS gives such a parent, as the row rule above says, it is as wide as each layer it holds, so a `max-width` of `100%` or more never limits the box and drops, as the `max-w-full` of Pagination's row does. Any other share of a parent's auto layout, such as `w-1/2`, stays unmapped, since a fill takes no share. A variant root sits in a component set without auto layout, so its percentage width takes the preview width its manifest names, as a fixed length, `w-full` the whole of it and `w-1/2` half, and stays unmapped without one, except on an in-flow block-level root with no margins, padding or border across, whose `100%` and unset width are one width (CSS 2.1 §10.3.3), which hugs, as Pagination's root does. A root's percentage height takes the preview height its manifest names, as the Sidebar container's `h-full` takes its demo frame's `h-[28rem]`, and stays unmapped without one. A preview height that changes by density names its CSS length, and a whole `100%` then binds what that length binds, as the Number field stepper's `h-full` binds `field-box-content-h`. A block-level root, one whose `display` is `block`, `list-item`, `flex`, `grid` or `flow-root`, that sets no width takes the preview width too, as a block box with no width fills its container, so Meter's `flex flex-col` root is 256px wide. No class makes a `<p>` or a `<div>` block-level, since the browser's default style sheet does, and the capture records that `display` from the user agent's origin, as on Accordion's panel and the `<div>` inside it that holds its text. It applies on the box below the base layer and every class, so a class's own `display` beats it, and the coverage table names it `user agent style sheet, p` or `user agent style sheet, div`. An `<input>` takes `inline-block` (HTML, Rendering §15.5), which a box that holds only its own text reads as a block box does inside it (CSS Display 3 §2.1). Field's description, error and legend fill their 384px this way. Beside a length that hugs or fills, `min-width`, `max-width`, `min-height` and `max-height` become the box's limits. On 2026-10-23 a real file capped a hugging column that clips at its `maxHeight` and exported it clipped (N10). Base UI writes a popup's `--available-height` and `--available-width` on its positioner, and the kit draws each popup with room, so neither caps anything: `max-h-(--available-height)` and `max-w-(--available-width)` drop, and a `min()` beside it caps at its other length, as Combobox's list caps at 252px. Figma takes only a positive limit, so a maximum of zero or less stays unmapped there. A part that holds nothing in a cell, paints nothing, and sets no length, padding or margin down the column that stacks it with no gap, draws in no layer there, as the Combobox popup's list while no row matches: CSS draws it 0 tall, and Figma keeps an auto-layout frame with no children at its last height (PE4). That holds for a part whose own text is all it holds, too, as the Command palette's `Command.Empty` holds its message only while no row matches. A border width paints whichever property sets it, `border-width`, a physical side or a logical one, so a bordered empty part stays drawn. A part at `opacity: 0` whose top margin is minus its height, in a column with no gap, draws in no layer either, a frame or an instance, as the collapsed Sidebar group's label instance (`h-8 -mt-8 opacity-0`) does. Such a part paints nothing and takes no room. Beside a fixed length, CSS lowers the length to its `max-*` and then raises it to its `min-*` (CSS 2.1 §10.4), and the kit check refuses a limit on a fixed axis, so the build folds the limits into the length: `w-[20px] max-w-[12px]` draws 12px wide. A limit that never moves the length drops, as the checkbox's `max-w-4` beside its `size-4` does. Whether one limit moves the length depends on the length and on the other limit, so when any of them binds a variable or is no literal, every limit on that axis stays unmapped. Limits that all bind the variable the fixed length binds never move it in any mode, so they drop, as the md control size's `min-h-(--control-h-md)` beside its `h-(--control-h-md)` does. A fold that leaves a length of zero or less stays unmapped too. A zero minimum, such as `min-w-0`, drops, since Figma takes no zero limit and its auto layout never shrinks a layer. `field-sizing: content` reads as a height that hugs its content. `overflow: hidden` makes a box clip its content. Every root clips already, except a root that casts no shadow and names `rootOverflow: "visible"`, as `Select (open)` does so that its popup's shadow shows past it, and as `Tooltip content` does so that its arrow shows below it. Radio row, Radio group, Toggle group, Text field, Select field, Textarea field, Number field and Avatar group name it too: CSS leaves each root unclipped, and the real file's compare on 2026-10-27 cut a ring a layer draws at the root's edge, a radio's or a control's, where the root clipped. A root that casts a shadow must clip, since Figma draws its spread only from a frame that clips (PR2), so Input group, whose root draws the field's own ring, clips and cuts its caret's invalid ring 1px past its top and bottom, which its coverage rows list. Those rows measure reach past a root's top and bottom only, since the build holds a layer's height in each mode but not its width, so a ring that crosses only a side edge, as a radio's focus ring at a row's left, shows in the compare and not in the coverage. An instance of such a set leaves its layers unclipped in place too.

A `position: fixed` root sits in a viewport its definition declares in `viewport`, as Dialog's popup, the Command palette and the overlay scrim sit in the browser window. A fixed box's containing block is the viewport (CSS 2.1 §10.1), so the root's percentages take the viewport's width and height, and its `dvh` and `dvw` read against it (CSS Values 4 §6.1.2), which a static frame holds still. A `calc()`, `min()` or `max()` that mixes percentages with lengths resolves against the viewport too, in a length and in a `min-*` or `max-*`, while against a preview width it stays unmapped: Dialog's `w-full max-w-(--overlay-width)`, with `--overlay-width: min(28rem, 90%)`, is 448px wide in a 1280 × 800 viewport and 360px in a 400 × 600 one, and its `max-h-[calc(100%-2rem)]` caps it at 768px or 568px; the palette's `w-[calc(100%-2rem)] max-w-xl` is 576px and its `max-h-[calc(100dvh-2*max(--spacing(4),12dvh))]` 608px. Opposing insets with no size of their own size the root to the viewport less the insets (CSS 2.1 §10.3.7, §10.6.4), so the scrim's `fixed inset-0` is the viewport's size. A fixed root's other offsets and its translate drop, as an absolute root's do. A root with no declared viewport leaves such lengths unmapped.

An instance part whose inner set's root is fixed in a declared viewport takes that viewport as its containing block in place too, where the composite's root declares the same viewport and the part's parent is the composite's root, its box the viewport (fixed at its top left and as large as it, by `inset-0` or its width and height), a flex box that centres the part on both axes and pads neither, as Alert dialog's open example, the scrim over the viewport, centres the popup. CSS centres a fixed box in the viewport whatever the composite pads, while a Figma frame centres its layers in the room its padding leaves, so a composite that pads an axis it centres on leaves the place unmapped, and so does the top-inset form below where it pads across. A root smaller than the viewport, or a frame below the root that centres the part, centres it in its own box rather than on the viewport, so it leaves the place unmapped too, its rows naming the reason. Its `fixed top-1/2 left-1/2` and `-translate-1/2` put its centre on the viewport's centre, which that centring draws, so they read as literal, and its `z-index` drops. A fixed instance at a top inset, `top-*` with `left-1/2 -translate-x-1/2`, reads the same way where it is the first part of a column that packs at its start, centres across and pads its top by that inset in the viewport, as the Command dialog's scrim places the palette 60px down. A fixed instance placed any other way leaves its place unmapped. An inner root built in place clips as its own set does.

`supports-backdrop-filter:` holds in every cell, since the compare page's Chromium meets the query (CSS Conditional 3 §6) and Figma draws a background blur, so the scrim's `backdrop-blur-xs` reads. A `backdrop-filter` of `blur(Npx)` and nothing else becomes the box's `backgroundBlur`, a radius twice the CSS length, since CSS's `blur()` takes the Gaussian's standard deviation (Filter Effects 1 §12.2) and Figma's radius counts twice that: a real file's BACKGROUND_BLUR of radius 8 blurred a black and white step to a Gaussian of σ about 3.45 with tails near ±8px, where CSS's `blur(4px)` is σ 4, read the effect back exactly and listed no instance overrides (W5-P13). `-webkit-backdrop-filter` reads with it, and `isolation` drops, since Figma draws a box's layers in its own order already.

A flex item whose own `align-self` is `stretch`, whose length across its parent's direction is unset or `auto` and whose margins across are not `auto`, as Toolbar's separator, is as long across as the flex line less its margins (CSS Flexbox 1 §9.4 step 11), which a layer that fills that axis draws, in a single-line flex box only. `components/box-sizing.ts` gives it a room that fills across, and `components/margin-box.ts` draws its margins as the padding of a frame that fills across in the parent's direction and hugs the item along it, with the item filling it. A stretched item that sets its own length across keeps it, as CSS does, and its `align-self` stays unmapped.

An item of a row with a fixed height stretches down the same way where neither it nor the row sets an alignment, since `align-self: auto` takes the row's `align-items: normal`, which stretches a flex item (CSS Box Alignment 3 §6.1), as List detail's root stretches its list and its pane to 320px. A row that hugs its height keeps its items' own heights, since the kit check refuses a layer that fills a hugging parent beside none that sets its height, as Radio row's label would.

A label is its box's own text, which `components/label-layout.ts` maps. In a block box, one that is neither flex nor grid, CSS sets the text in lines across the content width. Where that width is fixed or fills, text that wraps breaks its lines there, so the label fills the width and its height follows its lines. A flex box sets its text as an anonymous flex item, which does not grow, so a short label sits at its own width where the box places it. A label that fills draws the same only where the box places it where the text sets its lines: at the start of a row with its lines at the left, or centered with centered lines. A column with an unset `align-items` stretches its text across a definite width, which a fill always draws. A label that is the first of two layers a row of definite width spaces between its ends, with its lines at the left and a layer of fixed width after it, fills the room that layer and the row's gap leave: the item sits at the row's start, shrinks to that room and wraps there (CSS Flexbox 1 §9.7), as a vertical Navigation menu trigger's title does beside its caret. Where a TEXT property sets the label, it fills there whatever its sample's word count, as the Dropdown menu sub trigger's one-word `Share` does. The text must be free to wrap, with its lines at the left, and the layer after it must have a fixed width; #792 tracks proving that layer cannot shrink. A designer can type a label of several words, which then wraps between words inside the room in CSS and Figma alike. A centered or unwrapping label keeps the rules below. The gap is the least space between the two under `justify-between` (CSS Box Alignment 3 §8), and a filling text leaves nothing to space out, so the row packs its layers at the start with its gap between them. On 2026-10-23 a real file put a filling text flush against the caret under SPACE_BETWEEN, with no gap (PV). The builder measures no text, so a label whose longest word is wider than its room draws wrapped in Figma, where CSS keeps the word whole and pushes the other layer out. A text that never wraps keeps its own width there, as the next rule says. Any other placement, any other label that shares a row with other layers, and a label in a grid stay unmapped, and the coverage table says why. A text of ASCII letters and digits alone, such as Avatar's initials `AL`, holds no soft wrap opportunity, since line breaking keeps its classes AL and NU together (UAX #14), so a browser never wraps it. In a flex box such a label sits at its own width wherever the box places it, so where the rules above leave a wrapping label unmapped, it hugs instead. The one exception is a label a TEXT property sets in the spaced two-layer row above, which fills. A space, a hyphen, any other character, or a box that sets `word-break`, `line-break`, `hyphens` or `overflow-wrap` keeps those rules. `overflow-wrap` inherits (CSS Text 3 §5.5), so every label reads the one its box sets or inherits. A text that hugs its width with no `maxWidth`, in a box nothing bounds as the row rule says, sets each line at its max-content width, where every word fits, so the declaration drops there. A text of fixed or filling width under `break-word` or `anywhere`, as a `<textarea>`'s default style sets, breaks a word wider than its line, as a Figma text of that width wraps one, so the declaration maps there. That rests on the wrapping a label wider than its room shows in Figma, which the paragraph above names, and awaits a real-file probe (#522). It stays unmapped on any other text. A box with no text in it or below it drops it. Fullwidth letters such as `ＡＬ` count as other characters, since they take class ID, which breaks between them. A designer who types a text with a space into such a label sees one line where a browser would wrap it. A box that hugs its width is as wide as its text on one line, since a kit frame always has room to spare, so its label hugs. A box that hugs its width up to a literal `max-width` is as wide as its text up to that width, raised to its `min-width` where the minimum is wider (CSS 2.1 §10.4), less its padding and border, where the text wraps (CSS Sizing 3 §5.1). The cap passes down through every part that hugs its width. In a row the other layers and the gaps between them take their share of it, a fixed part its width and an icon its size, and a part's own left and right margins take theirs, so a label inside takes what is left as the text's `maxWidth`. On 2026-10-22 a real file wrapped a hugging text at its `maxWidth` and kept it hugging (P7). Empty's title and description wrap at the header's 384px this way. A box whose width is fixed or fills stops the cap, since it sets the width of what it holds. A cap the build cannot read in pixels stops the build where a wrapping label needs it: a bound `max-width`, a margin that is no literal, or a layer beside the label whose width the build does not know before it maps it, such as a part that hugs. So does a cap of 0 or less, since Figma takes only a positive `maxWidth`. `white-space: nowrap` keeps the text on one line at its own width, so the label hugs, as every published label does. `text-wrap: balance` and `pretty` choose where a wrapping text breaks its lines, and a Figma text fills each line before it starts the next, so both drop on a label that wraps, as Field legend's `text-balance` does, on one that wraps at a cap, and on one that hugs its one line. `white-space` and `text-wrap` are shorthands, and the cascade splits them into their longhands (CSS Text 4 §3.1 and §6.1): `white-space` sets `white-space-collapse` and `text-wrap-mode`, and `text-wrap` sets `text-wrap-mode` and `text-wrap-style`. Both set `text-wrap-mode`, which says whether the text wraps, so the declaration the cascade puts last wins: the one nearer the label, since an element inherits a property only where nothing nearer declares it, and on one element the later rule, as Tailwind emits `whitespace-nowrap` after `text-balance`. So Empty's title and description read the root's `text-balance`, and its Buttons' own `whitespace-nowrap` keeps their labels on one line under the content's `text-balance`, whose style then drops. A `text-wrap-style` on a label the mode keeps on one line drops too. `white-space-collapse: collapse` changes nothing in characters that hold no run of white space or line break, so it drops, and characters that hold one stay unmapped.

A part can show an image, as an `<img>` shows its `src`. Its definition names the bytes of a PNG file, the one format the kit ships, and the build writes them base64 encoded into the part's fill as an image paint, with their SHA-1 in lowercase hex. On 2026-10-22 a real file named Avatar's stored portrait by exactly that hash (probe P9b), so the plugin compares a held paint with it and Plan stores no image. `object-fit: cover` scales the image to cover the box and crops what overflows, which an image paint at FILL draws. Any other fit stays unmapped, and a box with no `object-fit` stops the build, since CSS's initial `fill` stretches the image, which no image paint draws. The image covers the box, so the build reads no `background-color` under it, and one the box sets stays unmapped. The kit's one picture is `components/portrait.png`, the docs Avatar demo's SVG portrait drawn once as a 96px PNG, twice the largest Avatar, since Figma stores no SVG image. Avatar's image and Item's image media both show it. `portrait.ts` says how it was drawn, and the compare renderers read the same file.

`truncate` sets `overflow: hidden`, `text-overflow: ellipsis` and `white-space: nowrap`. `text-overflow` applies only to a block box, so there, at a fixed or filling width, the label fills the width and ends in an ellipsis after one line. Chromium keeps a line's first character however narrow the box and sets the ellipsis after it, both clipped at the box, where a Figma text that ends in an ellipsis drops every character that does not fit beside it. So the label ends in an ellipsis only where the capture measured the box with room for its first character and the ellipsis (`ellipsisFits`); elsewhere `text-overflow` stays unmapped and the label hugs its one line, as on the collapsed Sidebar rail, whose row clips it where its span ends. A flex or grid box clips its text item with no ellipsis, so its label hugs and the box clips it. `line-clamp-2` makes the box a `-webkit-box` column that hides its overflow and clamps its wrapping text at `-webkit-line-clamp: 2`, so the label fills the width and ends in an ellipsis after two lines. One decision covers the box and its label: the build draws such a box as a column that sets its text from the top left only where every one of those declarations holds and the box holds just its label, and leaves the box's display and the clamp unmapped together otherwise. `-webkit-line-clamp` and `-webkit-box-orient` take effect only in a `-webkit-box`, so where a later `display` replaces it, as the trigger's `*:data-[slot=select-value]:flex` replaces the `line-clamp-1` before it on Select's value, both drop. In a box that hugs its width the text never overflows, so the ellipsis drops. A box that hugs its width up to a `max-width` cuts its text off at that limit, while a text in a hugging Figma frame keeps its own width past the frame's limit (P7), so a truncating or clamping label there stays unmapped, and so does a wrapping one under a `max-width` that is no literal length. `white-space`'s longhands and `text-align` inherit, so a `truncate` on a box keeps a part's label on one line, while the clip and the ellipsis stay on the box, and a box without text of its own drops its `text-overflow` and `-webkit-line-clamp`. Any other `white-space`, and an ellipsis without the other two declarations, stays unmapped. Where a box caps a wrapping label at a `max-width` the build cannot read in pixels as the label's room, as a literal cap less a padding that binds a variable, the label stays unmapped, unless the capture measured the label on one line there, as Tooltip's popup holds its text far inside 320px: then the label hugs on one line.

A box can hold its label in an element of its own, the box's child that holds the text, as a Sidebar row holds its text in a `<span>`. The box's `[&>span:last-child]:` classes style that span where the label ends the box. Tailwind compiles them to `.… > span:last-child`. A flex item is blockified (CSS Display 3 §2.7), so the span is a block container that takes its own `text-overflow`, and a span that hides its overflow shrinks below its text (CSS Flexbox 1 §4.5). A span does not grow (CSS Flexbox 1 §7.1), so last in a row that packs its layers at the start, with none growing, and setting its text from the start, the span is as wide as its text up to the room the row leaves it, so the label fills that room, and `truncate` ends it in an ellipsis after one line. The row then packs its layers at its start, since its alignment shows only where they overflow it, as the icon and gap of a collapsed Sidebar row overflow the rail's square. In a row that hugs its width with no cap on it the label hugs, and the ellipsis drops. A span the capture measured with no room for its first character and the ellipsis leaves its label hugging its one line past the span's edge, so the span's `overflow` draws only in a row that clips where the span ends, with no end padding or border, as the large collapsed Sidebar row does, and stays unmapped elsewhere. Anywhere else the span's `overflow`, `text-overflow` and `white-space` stay unmapped: a centered, end-aligned or justified text, where a filling text would set the characters across the row, and a row that hugs its width up to a cap, where CSS cuts the span off and a text in a hugging Figma frame keeps its own width (P7). Where no label sits in a `<span>` that ends the box, the classes stay unmapped.

`text-align` sets a label's lines at the left, in the center or at the right of its box. `start` and `end` read as left and right, and as right and left where the label sets or inherits `direction: rtl` (CSS Text 3 §6.1). The `direction` row stays unmapped, since a right-to-left box also orders its layers from the right, which the build does not draw. No class says where a native control places its text, such as an `<input>` that centers its value down its box, so the build takes the placement from the captured element's tag, as HTML renders the control, across as `start`, `center` or `end` and down as `top`, `center` or `bottom`. The label hugs its height, so the placement down sets the box's alignment, which moves the label to the top, the middle or the bottom of the box. The placement across sets the box's alignment too, which places a label that hugs its width. A label that fills the width also takes it as its own text alignment, and the box's alignment keeps the text in place when a designer makes it hug. A `text-align` still sets the lines across. A label with neither sets its lines from the top at its start, the left unless it runs right to left, as CSS sets a box's text. A block box of fixed or filling width whose label hugs, because its text never wraps, places the text where `text-align` sets its lines (CSS Text 3 §6.1), so the box's alignment along its row is MIN for lines at the left, as Code's `<pre>` sets its lines, CENTER for centered lines and MAX for lines at the right.

A placement's `lines` says the control sets its text in lines itself, whatever the box's `display` says. The text is no line box or anonymous item of the box's own, so the rules above do not apply to it.

- `single` sets the text on one line across the content box, as an `<input>` sets its value, whatever `white-space` says. The label fills the width and ends in an ellipsis after one line, the overflow the owner chose where a browser clips the value, and the box's `white-space` longhands, `text-overflow` and `-webkit-line-clamp` drop.
- `wrap` wraps the text at the content box's width, as a `<textarea>` wraps its value. The label fills the width with its height following its lines, and ends in no ellipsis. A `<textarea>` honours its own `white-space`, so `white-space: normal` and the browser's default `pre-wrap` read as the wrap, its `text-wrap-mode` as the wrap and its `white-space-collapse`, `collapse` or `preserve`, as dropped, since a Figma text draws the characters as written, and any other value stays unmapped, as do `text-overflow` and `-webkit-line-clamp`.

A box that hugs its width stops the build, since such a control takes its width from its `size` or `cols` attribute, which no Figma field holds.

`letter-spacing` in `em` is a share of the font size, which Figma's `letterSpacing` holds in `PERCENT`: `tracking-tight`, `-0.025em`, is -2.5%, as on Empty's title. On 2026-10-23 a real file read -2.5% back as written and narrowed an 18px text by the spacing (PL). CSS computes an `em` spacing where it is declared and a child inherits that length (CSS Text 4 §8.2), so a spacing a label inherits maps only where its font size is the declaring element's, and stays unmapped at another size. A spacing in any other unit stays unmapped, and a label with none gets no field, so it keeps the font's own spacing.

A unitless `line-height` is a factor of the element's own font size (CSS Inline 3 §4.4). Over a literal size it maps to pixels, as Item's `text-sm leading-snug` title draws 19.25px lines. Over a size a variable sets per mode, as Alert's `text-(length:--label-text)/snug` and `/normal` set `label-text`, it maps to Figma's line height in `PERCENT`, 137.5% and 150%, which follows the bound size at each density. On 2026-10-23 a real file kept a percent as written and drew a text bound to `label-text` 21, 24 and 21px tall at 150% in the Dense, Comfortable and Auto modes (PH).

A label shows one text in every cell, which a TEXT property of the set can set, or a text per cell through `charactersFor`, which no property sets, since a TEXT property holds one default. Labels on two parts can name two properties. Two labels that name one property list it once and must start with the same text, and the build stops on two different texts and names both labels.

A part can be optional behind a BOOLEAN property of the set, as a docs demo leaves out a description. Its manifest names the property and the value it starts with in `optional`. The set lists the property once, and the part's frame links its `visible` to it, so a designer shows or hides the part from the properties panel. Two parts that name one property must start it the same, and the build stops on two defaults and names both parts. An icon can be optional the same way, as a menu row shows its icon only where a demo gives it one: its slot names the property in `optional`, and the icon layer links its `visible` to it beside its swap property.

A part is optional only where showing it changes no style outside its own layers. For each optional part and each axis that shows or hides one, the capture of a cell with the part and of the same cell without it are compared element by element outside the part: each element's winning declarations and its pseudo-elements', not its position, since Figma's auto layout gives back the room a hidden layer takes. An axis stays an axis where any other box's padding, gap, size rule or paint changes with it, as Button's padding reads `has-data-[icon=…]` and Item's media aligns to a description through `item-described:`. A state whose only drawn effect is an indicator, as a selected Select row's check or a checked menu row's indicator, stays an axis too, since it is the component's state rather than a part a designer adds.

A part can render another kit component, as Alert renders a Button for its action. Its manifest, an `InstanceManifest` among a box's `parts`, names the inner set's manifest in `of`, the inner cell for each outer cell, and the values it gives the inner set's TEXT, BOOLEAN and INSTANCE_SWAP properties by name in `properties`. It names the cells that render it too, and can name a BOOLEAN of the outer set in `optional` and mark the instance `exposed`. The set draws the part as an instance layer of the inner set's variant for the inner cell, and the build proves the instance draws what Fuse draws there (`components/instance-parts.ts`):

- The build maps the inner root in place, below the outer parts, which pass it their inherited values and their child, part and `**:` classes as they pass a part theirs. The inner root and the elements below it take the inner set's declarations where its own capture shows them in the same inner cell, and the composite's otherwise. The mapping shows each TEXT value as the characters of the label its property sets, and each INSTANCE_SWAP value as the icon its property picks, and leaves the inner definition as it is, so a part keeps the identity the inner set's own build gave it, as a Switch thumb stays the slot around a dot it is in the Switch's set.
- `components/instance-diff.ts` diffs that root against the inner set's variant, layer by layer. Characters that differ on a label a TEXT property sets, and an icon that differs where an INSTANCE_SWAP property picks it, become property values. A BOOLEAN value that differs from the property's default becomes one too, and the root keeps every layer its variant holds, as the inner set's own property only hides a part.
- A fill, a stroke's color, a text's color, a glyph's color, a corner and shadows that differ become overrides at the layer's key path. The root's fill is the one its ring needs, as the plugin writes it.
- A solid stroke on some sides becomes an override of its four weights, beside its color where that differs too, where the inner variant's stroke is solid with literal weights and both take their room in the layout alike, as a chip segment's `border-0 border-s` keeps the start edge of Button's 1px border. A dashed stroke, a bound weight and a stroke only one of the two draws stay unmapped. The plugin writes the four weights on the instance's layer. On 2026-11-20 a real file listed each side a write left unlike the main's, the top one as `stokeTopWeight`, never `strokeWeight`, and kept a side listed after the main's weight was written back on it (SW). So an override the spec drops goes back to the main's stroke as the inner variant now draws it, its four weights or its one weight, bound or dashed, and the next Plan, which reads them equal, lists nothing. No real file has run the plugin's write yet, so the owner's Apply is its check.
- A text's face becomes an override where both texts take a literal weight in one family, as a chip operator's `font-normal` shows Regular inside a Button whose label is Medium. The plugin writes the face on the instance's text as one font in the main label's family, resolved in the instance's modes, whatever the text holds: a family a designer gave it, a range of another, or the style a TEXT write reset it to (G5). The write keeps the family's binding where the family stays and drops it where it changes (G8). It puts the glyph ranges back in that face, on a label no TEXT property sets too. The face is literal, so the write also goes on a label that shows it already and binds a weight, and drops that binding (PG8). The face goes first among a label's overrides, so a later write on the label needs only the face. Before the first write, the planning pass loads, for each label the run writes, every font the label holds now, the face in the main's family, and in the glyph font's family where the label shows glyphs. A label whose weight the run binds again takes the weight's face in each family it holds, since a binding keeps each character's family (PG13). No face is loaded in a family the run does not write, so a character in a family without the face needs none. No probe has read what a TEXT write restores once a range covers every character, and the face write does not depend on it. A face a bound weight picks stays unmapped. On 2026-11-20 a real file listed `fontName` on the instance's text after a font write and kept it listed after the main's face was written back (SW), so a face the spec drops goes back to the main's, with its glyph ranges, or to the main's weight binding where the main now binds one, and the next Plan lists nothing. No real file has run the plugin's write yet, so the owner's Apply is its check.
- The root's alignment along its auto layout's direction that differs becomes an override where the instance fills its parent along that direction and nothing else about it stays unmapped, as a Button that fills its row with `justify-start` draws its label at the start. The alignment places the root's layers, so beside a difference that stays unmapped it stays unmapped too, as in a Dropdown menu row whose shortcut margin does (#773). On 2026-11-19 a real file listed `primaryAxisAlignItems` in such an instance's `overrides` and kept MIN through a variant switch, a TEXT property change, a density switch, where the label followed the bound padding from 16px to 24px, and nesting in another component's instance resized from 240px to 320px wide (LD1). That alignment on an instance that hugs or takes a fixed length along its direction, or on a frame below the root, stays unmapped, since the probe covered neither.
- A text's alignment across its lines that differs becomes an override too, on the text, which a real file kept through the main's alignment and character edits on 2026-10-24, and kept listed after a write equal to the main's (NA). A label a TEXT property sets can take more lines than the composite shows, so the override holds wherever the alignment differs, as Empty's `text-center` reaches its Button labels, which the kit's Button set draws at the left, since it does not model the user agent's `text-align: center` on a `<button>`. An alignment that differs down the text stays unmapped.
- The root's sizing in the outer layout is the instance's, so an axis can fill, take a fixed length or hug, as a column stretches its control and a row sizes a block-level root to its content where its own set gives it the preview width. On 2026-10-23 a real file drew an instance at FILL, at a fixed width and hugging (N4). The layers inside guard the sizing, since a layer that sizes against the root's length draws another way once that length changes, which the diff leaves unmapped.
- A glyph the inner root turns half a turn in place, where the variant's is unturned and an INSTANCE_SWAP property picks it, becomes a swap to its half-turn mirror, since Figma refuses a turn on a layer inside an instance (PT). `components/glyph-mirrors.ts` lists each pair, CaretDown and CaretUp: the mirror's shape is the glyph's shape turned 180° about its centre. Every other turn stays unmapped.
- `properties` can give TEXT values to instances inside the inner root in `nested`, each at the keys of the instance parts down to it, as a Command list row gives its shortcut's caps `Alt` and a letter. The build applies them in place, and the diff carries an exposed instance inside the root that differs only in TEXT values, its own or those inside it, as nested values at its key path. Figma set such a value through exposure two levels down on 2026-10-25, and kept it through the outer instance's own writes and variant switches (PN). Every other difference inside, and one through an instance that is not exposed, stays unmapped.
- A label a TEXT property sets in place lays out as its variant does, since Figma takes a text layer's sizing from the main and only its characters from the property. Outside the spaced two-layer row the label rule names, a text with no break opportunity keeps its own width there only where the variant's text does too, so a one-word Command list row takes its two-word variant's filling layout, which draws it as CSS does. A variant that keeps one word at its own width, given text that can wrap, lays out another way, which stays unmapped.
- A box with fewer than two layers in its auto layout in both trees draws no gap, since a gap sits only between adjacent items (CSS Box Alignment 3 §8), so its `itemSpacing` is no difference there, as Input group's addon button holds one icon beside its `gap-2`. A layer a BOOLEAN hides counts, since a designer can show it.
- An instance takes everything else from its main component (N1 to N4). So any other difference, such as a padding, a gap, a type field or the layers a box holds, lands in the coverage table as unmapped. Its row names the field, both values, the inner variant and the fix: an axis of the inner set that draws it, or the part drawn as a frame. An instance has no margin box, so its margins draw in a frame around it, as a part's margins paragraph below says. A position other than `relative` stays unmapped, except an absolute instance at its parent's end and, down, either a fixed distance below its top or centred, as an overlay's corner close sits at `absolute top-4 right-4` and a Sidebar menu badge's `absolute right-1 top-[calc(var(--row-h)/2-0.625rem)]` sits over its row. Its `right` is MAX's end inset. A `top` that is one length in every mode, beside a parent's top border that is one too, is MIN's top inset, the border and the `top` together, since CSS offsets the box from the padding edge and Figma places it from the frame's top edge. Otherwise its `top` is CENTER's offset of its centre from the parent's centre where that offset is one length in every mode: the build adds the parent's top border, the `top`, half the instance's height and minus half the parent's height as affine lengths over the synced variables, and the variables must cancel, as `row-h / 2 − 10px` beside a 20px badge in an item as tall as `row-h` leaves 0. The parent must be positioned and as tall as its one layer in flow. Figma keeps that inset and that offset as the instance and its parent resize, a density switch included (N11). Any other `top`, `bottom` or `left` leaves the position and offsets unmapped, with the reason. The parent's `relative` still drops, and its coverage row names the anchored instance and its constraints as what it anchors. No kit set draws MAX × MIN yet: Dialog's corner close will. A real file kept such an instance's end and top insets through parent and instance resizes (W5-P12).
- An instance part can name the `data-slot` the outer component stamps on the inner root in `slot`, as `Popover.Trigger` stamps `popover-trigger` on its Button. The layer keeps the inner root's name, and the build finds the element by that slot.
- The in-place diff reads a text that fills across in the inner variant, a direct child of an inner root the instance hugs, as the hugging text the root builds in place: on 2026-10-25 Figma drew such a text at its own width, following its TEXT property (PH2). A popup's widest row is such an instance. The reverse reads too: a root built in place that a column stretches across, or fits to its text up to the column's width, fills there, and so does its one text, where the inner variant's root and text hug, as a Date range picker's `w-fit` Label instance holds `Stay`. Where the root paints nothing, a background blur included, and its text starts from the left, holds no soft wrap opportunity and sits under no `word-break`, `line-break`, `hyphens` or `overflow-wrap`, its own or one it inherits, CSS sets the text on one line at its own width whatever the room (CSS Sizing 3 §5.1). So the instance hugs as the variant's root does where the column places a layer that hugs at its start, in CSS and in Figma: a column whose unset `align-items` stretches the root, a grid in one column or a box of blocks, whose Figma layout packs at its start, beside no auto margin across. A root that grows along a row, or that `self-stretch` stretches in a column that ends its layers, keeps the difference unmapped, and so does one whose clip can cut the word. CSS clips the word at a stretched width, and the instance, which clips as its variant does, clips it at a widest width built in place or held by the variant. A `fit-content`, `max-content` or `min-content` root under no `max-width` is at least as wide as its one line (CSS Sizing 3 §5.2), so its own clip cuts nothing. `settleInstanceRoot` settles the instance's sizing before the diff walks it. A label a designer types with spaces wraps in CSS past the column's width and runs on in the instance, as it does in the inner set's own variant. Every other inner sizing difference stays unmapped (`components/hugging-rows.ts`).

The build makes the sets in the registry's order and hands each set the sets before it, so an inner definition that holds instances itself builds first, and its own instances build in place inside the composite's. It stops, naming them, on an instance part that shows a set the registry lists later or not at all, and on instance parts that form a cycle.

Every declaration carries its origin, the set or component that owns its rows. The inner component's own classes are the inner set's coverage, so the composite lists none of their rows that map. It lists each class of its own that reaches the instance: a class the outer component passes, and a class of an outer box that styles the inner root or a layer below it, such as a `*:data-[slot=…]:` class, whatever the layers are named. Each read names every spec field it fills, on the layer at its key path from the inner root, and the composite's row follows the diff of those fields. One declaration can fill several: `place-content` both alignments, a column's `align-items` the sizing of each layer it stretches, and the root's `display`, lengths and limits the sizing of the layers inside, which the instance's own sizing does not carry. A read in place routes its row to the instance that holds the reading layer, so an outer `[--p:16px]` that an inner `var(--p,8px)` padding reads lands with that padding's difference, and the declaration's origin still owns the row. A read that fills a field an override or the instance's sizing carries keeps its outcome, bound or literal, as a passed `[--radius-button:8px]` does through the Button's `rounded-(--radius-button)`. So does a margin the composite draws around the instance: a frame around it draws a margin of a length, and the holding row's auto-margin rules draw an auto margin, as Skip's `me-auto` sets it apart from Back and Next in Tour step's footer. Where the holding row is itself the inner root of an instance built in place, that instance's diff of the row's alignment and layers decides the margin's row. An outer `[--push:auto]` that a nested instance's `ms-[var(--push,0px)]` reads is unmapped where the row's instance cannot carry the spacing it gives the row. A read that fills a field no instance field carries is unmapped with the difference, and one whose fields the inner variant draws the same drops. Any read of the inner component's own classes in place that does not map lands as unmapped too, naming the instance. Each owner keeps logs of its own, so a class the outer component passes and the same class on the inner root stay apart: a passed `hover:` class no inner cell shows lands in the composite's coverage as dropped. A rule that styles child parts or every element below its box carries the entry that logs it, and the element that takes the rule marks that entry, as `components/coverage-log.ts` keeps them.

A frame part or an instance that sits in a box that stacks blocks follows the box's paint order, as `components/stacking.ts` decides it. The order reads what each block paints, with every instance below it, at any depth, drawn as its inner root built in place, so a block that holds a header instance or frame whose ring, or whose ringed instance's ring, meets a later block's fill puts its first layer on top, as it does for a frame. A root carries the `data-slot` its manifest's `elementSlot` gives each cell, as Pagination's edge carries `pagination-previous` or `pagination-next`, and none where `elementSlot` is unset. The in-place cascade gives an instance's inner root that slot too, so an outer `*:data-[slot=pagination-previous]:` class reaches a previous edge.

A label's `leadingSpace` says the element holds a space before the characters, as Meter's value holds one after its icon. A box that sets its text in lines puts that space between a start icon and the characters, one space advance of the label's font wide, which the build draws as the box's gap. Roboto's space is 508 of its 2048 units per em at Regular and 509 at Medium, as the `hmtx` tables of `@fontsource/roboto` 5.3.0 hold it, so Meter's 14px value sits 3.47px after its icon. With no icon before it the space starts the line, and in a flex box it starts the text's anonymous item, and either way it collapses, so the box keeps its own gap. The build stops where the space would sit beside more than one icon, or the label's size is no literal. A designer who retypes the value keeps the gap, which a space in the characters would not survive.

A part can carry a style attribute, as Base UI's `MeterIndicator` writes `inset-inline-start: 0; height: inherit; width: 85%` on a meter's fill. The capture records the attribute's declarations, and they cascade above every class, as a style attribute beats every rule of a style sheet, so the fill's `h-full` loses to them and leaves no row. `inherit` takes the parent's computed value, inherited or not, so the fill's height is the bar's 6px, and the coverage table records the bar's `h-1.5` with what the fill read. `inset-inline-start` and `inset-inline-end` read as `left` and `right`, where a left-to-right page puts them, and cascade with them, so the fill's `left-0` loses too. The coverage table lists each declaration under the part, with `inline style` in place of a class.

A part with `absolute top-1/2 left-1/2 -translate-x-1/2 -translate-y-1/2` centers itself on its containing block. The offsets put its corner at the block's center, and the -50% translate moves it back by half its own size. The build draws it as a layer its parent's auto layout centers, and requires the parent to center its one layer on both axes. The parent must also sit centered in the containing block, which the build does not check. Radio's indicator is a 16px box centered in its `relative` root, so the dot lands at the root's center, as in the browser.

An absolute part, a frame or an instance, sits outside its parent's auto layout by its distances to its parent's edges, where its parent is its containing block: positioned, or naming the positioned element around it with its border box (`components/absolute-placement.ts`). CSS takes an absolute box out of its parent's flow and resolves its offsets and percentages against its containing block, the padding box of its nearest positioned ancestor (CSS 2.1 §10.1, §10.3.7, §10.6.4), and Figma places a layer from its parent's outer edge, so each distance is the offset and the parent's border on that side. A static parent with no border also places the part where the capture measured, in every mode, the parent's border box as the padding box of a containing block above it (`parentSpansBlock`, `capture/parent-spans-block.ts`), as Sheet's column spans the popup that places its corner close. There each distance is the offset alone. The page reads the block's padding box from the part's own box, margins and used offsets (CSS 2.1 §10.3.7, §10.6.4) and checks it against the parent's border box and the nearest positioned ancestor's padding box. That ancestor must be the captured root or an element below it, which the set draws. Every element from the parent up to that ancestor must be the only element child of the one above it, overflow visibly and set no `clip-path`, since CSS paints the part over a later in-flow sibling and does not clip it there, where Figma would do both. None of them, the ancestor included, may generate a `::before` or `::after` box. Figma draws one as a layer of its element's frame beside the frame that holds the part, and no order of the two paints as CSS does for every such box (CSS 2.1 Appendix E). No element after the part in tree order inside the ancestor, outside the part's own subtree, may be positioned or form a stacking context, by a `z-index` on a flex or grid item, an opacity below 1, a transform, a filter, `isolation`, a blend mode, `will-change` or paint containment among others. CSS paints such a box over the part (CSS 2.1 Appendix E, CSS Positioned Layout 3), while the kit orders the part last in its parent's frame. Every element from the parent up to, but not including, the ancestor must declare a `width` and a `height` of `100%`, a `min-width` and a `min-height` of `0px`, no maximum, an `auto` flex basis and an `auto` aspect ratio, and none may be a grid item, whose percentages resolve against a track that can cap it. Each then has its container's size at any size of the block above the padding bound below (CSS 2.1 §10.2, §10.4, §10.5, §10.7). A column with `max-width: 220px` in a 200px block matches the block at the sample and past it by 10px, and at 250px CSS puts the close 14px past the column's end, so the page refuses it by its declaration. The ancestor's height must be definite: a length, a percentage of a definite height, or an absolute or fixed box's height, since a `100%` height against a height the content sets computes to `auto` (CSS 2.1 §10.5). The page then grows the ancestor by 10px across through `min-width` and reads the same, and again by 10px down through `min-height` alone. It grows one axis at a time, since a column with `aspect-ratio: 1` follows a block grown both ways and stays 200px tall in a 200px-wide block grown 100px down. As a known limit, a `100%` `border-box` column is never smaller than its padding plus its border, a bound that grows with the block where the padding is a percentage, so in a block narrower or shorter than that bound the column overflows the block and the part sits off its offsets from the column's edge. The page does not refuse such a block, since no designer draws a panel that small. Sheet content's column sets no padding or border, so its bound is zero. The build honours the fact only where the part's element is a child of its frame's element, with no undrawn wrapper between them. Toast's close records no span, since its root pads the content column that holds it. It draws in the root's frame instead, as the paragraph on `blockSteps` below says. The spec holds one `placement` for the layer, `edges` against its `parent`, with one place on each axis, each kept by a Figma constraint as the parent resizes:

- `start`: its start offset set and its end `auto`, at a length of its own, which MIN keeps, as a timeline item's dot sits at `absolute top-2 left-0`;
- `end`: the other way round, which MAX keeps, as a Sidebar menu badge sits 4px from its item's end; an overlay's corner close at `absolute top-4 right-4` keeps its place through the wrapper below;
- `stretch`: both offsets set at an `auto` length, or one offset at `100%` of the parent's length, which ends that far past the other edge, which STRETCH keeps, as a timeline item's connector reaches 32px past its item. An `inherit` length counts as the parent's whole one only where the parent has no limit, border or padding on the axis, since `inherit` takes the parent's declared length and a `min-height` can make the parent taller; elsewhere it is the declared length, a `start` or `end`. Offsets that leave no length, as `left-[60px] right-[60px]` in a 100px parent, where CSS draws the box empty (CSS 2.1 §10.3.7), leave the place unmapped;
- `scale`: a zero start offset at a share of a parent with no border or padding on the axis, whose length the build knows, which SCALE keeps, as a meter's fill keeps its share of its bar. SCALE keeps the start a share of the parent too, so a nonzero start, which CSS keeps fixed, leaves a share below the whole unmapped, and a whole share stretches;
- `centre`: for an instance down its parent, its centre one offset from the parent's centre in every mode, which CENTER keeps, as a Sidebar menu badge's `top-[calc(var(--row-h)/2-0.625rem)]` centres it on its row (N11).

Every offset, border and centre offset must be one length in every mode, with no synced variable left in it. A frame's own length on a `start`, `end` or `scale` axis is a literal, and an instance keeps the length its main gives it, so an instance takes no `stretch` or `scale`. A `min-*` other than zero or a `max-*` other than `none`, a zero maximum included, on a `stretch` or `scale` axis bounds a length CSS holds and the constraints would carry past (CSS 2.1 §10.4), so it leaves the place unmapped, and its row names the bound. A part that turns, moves by a translate or scales, an offset or length of any other shape, both offsets beside a length of the part's own, and a containing block the parent is not keep the part's place unmapped, an instance's with the reason. On 2026-10-22 a real file kept a SCALE × STRETCH layer's share through a FILL bar and an instance resize (P8).

MAX keeps a layer's end inset as its parent resizes. In the outer component's main, it also keeps the inset when a mode changes the layer's own length (W5-P14b). In an instance of the outer component, a mode that changes the layer's length keeps its `x` instead, so its end inset drifts by the change. That is how a designer places a Dialog, and how the previews show one. On 2026-11-19 a close bound to a density variable grew from 24 to 32px wide, and its right inset fell from 16 to 8px (PG14, PG14b). CSS keeps the declared offset whatever the box's length (CSS 2.1 §10.3.7).

So an instance at its parent's right and top, at a width a mode can change, draws in a placement wrapper (`components/placement-wrapper.ts`). The wrapper is a row with no fill and no clip. It is absolute and STRETCH across the parent at its full width, aligned to its end and padded there by the right distance. It sits at the parent's top, padded by the top distance, and hugs its height. The instance sits in the row's flow with no place of its own, and keeps its key and its BOOLEAN property. The wrapper's key is the instance's with ` placement` after it, and the wrapper takes the instance's slot in the paint order. On 2026-11-19 such a wrapper kept a close 16px from a column's right and top through resizes of the column, a density switch in an instance and a BOOLEAN toggle. The column's layout stayed as it was (PG16).

An axis needs no wrapper where the capture measured the part one length in every mode (`oneLength`). The Sidebar menu badge is one: no mode changes its text or padding. An instance a mode can resize at its parent's bottom, at a `centre`, or across beside a place other than the top leaves its place unmapped, since no probe has kept such a place.

An absolute instance part can also draw in the frame of its containing block where that block lies above its parent (`components/part-preparation.ts`, `capture/block-frame.ts`). The capture records on the part how many steps up its containing block sits (`blockSteps`), where in every mode the part paints over everything else in the block and keeps its offsets from the block's padding edge as the block grows. The JSDoc of `blockFrame` in `capture/block-frame.ts` lists each condition the page checks. The box whose element sits that many steps above the part's lifts the part out of the frames between and maps it as one of its own absolute parts, after the frame that held it, at the offset and the block's border on each side. The part keeps its place only where the block's order draws it as the topmost layer, and otherwise its `position` stays unmapped, with the reason. The box lifts instance parts only, enters no frame that a BOOLEAN property shows, and lifts no part whose key or placement wrapper's key one of its own layers already has. Where the capture also measured that the part's parent spans the block (`parentSpansBlock`), the part keeps its place in its parent's frame, as Sheet's corner close does. Where the kit draws no layer for that parent, or the parent draws a border, the span gives no place, and the block lifts the part by its steps instead. Toast's content column holds its close, and the root, the close's containing block, draws it in a placement wrapper padded 8px on the right and top, since density resizes the `icon-sm` button.

A `stretch` axis's length is what the parent leaves between the two distances. Where the build knows the parent's padding box, the length is a literal, so geometry that reads it resolves, as a `rounded-full` overlay's radius is half its shorter side. Where it does not, as beside a parent that hugs its text, the spec's sizing names the axis `placed`, the plugin sets the length, and nothing reads it: a radius that needs it stays unmapped. The build decides a part's place and the lengths it sets together, before the part maps, so a refused place leaves no length to it, and the kit check refuses a `placed` length that no `stretch` sets. Out of the flow, the part is no layer of its parent: every layout reader of the box, the `100%` and stretch rules above, the label's rules and the grid's one item, counts only the layers in its flow, as `outOfLayout` says for any placement. CSS paints a box's in-flow, non-positioned descendants first, then its positioned ones, absolute and `relative` alike, in tree order among themselves (CSS 2.1 Appendix E). Figma's order of a box's layers is both their paint order and their flow, so the in-flow layers keep their flow order, and each placed layer sits at the latest slot that paints it as CSS does: over every non-positioned in-flow layer and every positioned one before it in the tree, under every positioned one after it (`components/paint-order.ts`). An overlay over a static block before a `relative` one sits between them. Where a non-positioned layer flows after that slot, as a static block after a `relative` one that an overlay covers, no order paints the three as CSS does, so the absolute part's place stays unmapped, and a pseudo-element with no place draws no layer. Two in-flow layers paint in flow order, where CSS paints a `relative` one over a later static one, which shows only where they overlap. Figma has no stacking level, so a `z-index` stays unmapped, except on a child of the root that the capture measured, in every mode, painting over every other box of the root by it (`paintsLast`, `capture/paints-last.ts`). Its level is a positive integer that applies to it, as it is positioned or a flex or grid item, so it forms a stacking context, which paints its subtree as one, as a frame paints its layers. Nothing follows it in the root, no element, no text and no `::after` of the root, and no other box of the root, nor a `::before` or `::after` of one, has a higher level that applies. A box at the same level counts only where the part, that box and every element between them and the root compute `order: 0`, since a flex or grid container paints its items in order-modified document order (CSS Flexbox 1 §5.4.1, CSS Grid 1 §9.5), so an `order` can paint the earlier box last. The rule also refuses an `order` on a box that is no item, which leaves tree order as it is. CSS then paints the part after every other box of the root (CSS 2.1 §9.9.1 and Appendix E, step 9). The fact says nothing of Figma's order, and the build drops the level where the root's order also draws the part last. A flex item with a level and no position leaves an earlier absolute part's slot at the end, which paints over it in Figma, so its level stays unmapped there. As a known limit, where the root forms no stacking context, CSS also paints the part over a box the host lays after the root that overlaps it, which no instance can. Every other use of `position`, `top` or `left` stays unmapped, and a translate maps only as the next paragraphs say.

A turned part that hangs from its containing block's bottom edge, centred across it, as a Tooltip's arrow does, sits outside its parent's auto layout by its centre. `components/placement.ts` reads it where the part is absolute, its `left` is half the containing block's width plus a length, its `bottom` is a length, its translate moves it down only, its size is a literal, and it sets no `top`, `right` or margin. CSS offsets the box from its containing block's padding box (CSS 2.1 §10.3.7, §10.6.4), the translate moves it, a percentage taking its own size, and `rotate` turns it about its centre, which stays put (CSS Transforms 2 §6). So the centre sits a fixed distance from the block's horizontal centre and from its bottom edge, whatever the block's size: the arrow's `left: calc(50% - 5px)`, `bottom: -10px` and `translate-y-[calc(-50%-2px)]` put it on the centre, 2px above the edge. The spec's `placement` of kind `pivot` holds that centre, which CENTER across and MAX down keep. The parent must be the containing block: positioned, or static inside a positioned element with the box's border box, as Base UI's tooltip positioner holds its popup, and with no border. The kit check takes any turn on such a layer, since it sits outside the flow. The plugin (`plugin/src/pivot.ts`) writes `layoutPositioning` ABSOLUTE and the constraints before the box, then the box and the turn, then `x` and `y` last, computed from the centre, the turn and the parent's settled size through Figma's transform `[[cos r, sin r, x], [-sin r, cos r, y]]` (PQ). It compares the centre, never `x` and `y`.
